Documentation›Developer Internals›Function index

11 · Function and type index #

In one sentence: An address book for code names. It is not a list of functions your program executed.

Key terms #

TermMeaning
DefinitionThe place in a Rust or C file where a function or type is declared.
Call-flow linkA separate, reviewed arrow showing that one piece of code calls another, transfers data, or marks a conceptual boundary.

Example. Look up validate_deep here to open its source. Then read the validation chapter to learn why it distinguishes a corrupt trace from a partial one.

What this index is (and isn't) #

It covers production Stage 1 definitions, including important private helpers. For each, it gives the signature and source location as a starting point.

It isIt is not
A lookup of names, signatures and locationsA story of how a function gets invoked
Searchable by qualified name, type or pathAn explanation of each algorithm
An address book alongside the DOC-2/DOC-3 chaptersProof of a runtime call

Why it can't prove calls: source parsing alone can't see dispatch through traits (Rust's interfaces), callbacks, or another process.

Browsing #

Use the local search with a qualified function name, type or source path. Expand a symbol to see its signature and source reference.

  • For selected verified callers and callees, open the call-flow explorer.
  • Functions not connected in the curated graph are still listed here. See the existing engine handbook and parser handbook for longer explanations.

Template for future function walkthroughs #

A later, full walkthrough of one function must cover these ten points. Line-by-line paraphrase should be avoided.

#Cover…
1Purpose
2When and under what conditions it's invoked
3Inputs, preconditions and invariants
4The algorithm, in meaningful groups
5Important branches
6Borrowed versus owned state
7Result or emission, plus errors and partiality
8Downstream consumers
9Rationale (why it's built this way)
10The exact source reference

The metadata index is the scaffold, not an assertion that this prose is already complete.
